"blue card" is the answer. mr hans van baalen, who is the leader of the vvd delegation, has clearly resigned and has asked prime minister rutte to resign from the initiative. what i notice is that the coalition party in the netherlands apparently does not dare to do so. i've said that before. i was in amsterdam three days ago, introducing hans van mierlo's book 'the mad adventure' and saying that the silence of the catshuis - the residence of the prime minister in the hague in the netherlands - is in fact unacceptable. but the position of both liberal parties in my group has been clear and unambiguous, including the vvd and mr van baalen.
"blue card" question. what is it? i have listened to you very carefully, mr cornelissen. you compare the situation to the film "the lives of others", a film about the communist dictatorship in the former east germany, where people were spied on for their political opinions and where a different political opinion could lead to imprisonment, to loss of income, to consequences for the children, etc. do you think it is intelligent to compare this situation in a totalitarian society to, ochgottekes, a website that asks not to report the presence of eastern european foreigners, but criminal acts committed by foreigners? i think one is really not comparable to the other, or do you?
the union can and must play a role in stimulating this competitiveness, and the competitiveness of an economy does not lie solely in the cost of wages. we all agree. a trained workforce, living and working under social protection conditions, in a state where the private and public sectors invest in research, with cutting-edge transport and communication infrastructures, is a workforce that will contribute to the competitiveness of the union. we have been calling for months for a more sensible management of the crisis, focusing not only on budgetary savings but also on investment for growth, jobs and social inclusion. however, the economic governance texts continue to threaten wage indexation systems which are useful for maintaining internal consumption and collective labour agreements. unacceptable statements against the european social model are being made at the highest level. do you not think that all these ideas, particularly on certain social protections, on the budgets of the member states and on the union's budget, which have an undeniable added value and leverage effect, are in reality obstacles to the competitiveness we so much want?
i thank the commissioner for his answer and i think that it must be in the context of a dialogue that we find out what is best for certain countries, depending on what is happening historically in those countries and, perhaps, see if we cannot generalize what is happening in certain countries. perhaps as a complement to this question, and again in the same field, what do you think should be done to avoid imbalances in minimum income and minimum wage which should be established at european level?
of course, the restoration of competitiveness and economic growth does not happen with one measure. many measures must be taken. and of course, now - mr barnier has already referred to this - a huge amount of new regulation is being unleashed on the financial market. but small and mediumsized enterprises are still the real engine of economic growth in europe, and this new financial regulation has in many cases also had a negative effect on lending to these small and mediumsized enterprises. my question is very specific: how will the commission continue to meet the urgent need for credit for small and mediumsized enterprises? the second question concerns the internal market. it is clear that the ten-t and the connecting europe facility can play an important role in encouraging and strengthening the internal transport market. however, it is very difficult to convince member states to accept this project and there is a great deal of opposition from the council to the selection of a number of trans-european projects which are cross-border. how will the commission ensure that the european interest is also kept at the forefront in the ten-t and in the connecting europe facility?
of course, in the debate on transeuropean networks, some member states continue to raise barriers, including to the provision of capital to small and mediumsized enterprises. my question is very specific. the european commission has been given a number of new powers in the field of economic governance. it is also possible that it could use it to overturn a number of measures which are hindering growth, which member states sometimes propose today, also in the context of budgetary control. how can the european commission ensure that growth-enhancing measures are taken in these various budgets?
the european union is importing 80% of its cocoa from west africa. we are all aware of the many and countless reports on child labour. it's a worrying one. as a union we also provide the strongest chocolate industry. so we have a responsibility, we have to do something about these conditions. we must ensure that there are good conditions, both economically and socially. and it is good that we bring child labour into this debate. the resolution by mr moreira is a good one. it indicates one of two ways in which we can prevent child labour. one of those two ways is to ensure greater cooperation, by exchanging know-how, by ensuring that everyone works well together and indeed to ensure a distribution sector in which cocoa which is subject to child labour has no chance. but the second way, ladies and gentlemen, is to ensure fair prices, good prices. and the international cocoa agreement should do more about it, should have hands and teeth to do something about it. because let's face it, if we have good prices, people can earn their money. when there are low prices, there is poverty. poverty leads to child labour, so ensuring fair prices is an absolute necessity and is an element in this discussion.
in writing. once again, we are called upon to defend the working conditions of an entire sector. i would remind you that cocoa is one of the most sought-after commodities in the world after coffee and sugar and that the largest part of the value added in the production chain goes to the nordic countries. of the total cost of producing a chocolate bar, less than 5% goes to the countries of the south. so the big chocolate companies seem to have forgotten that they are entirely dependent on millions of small cocoa farmers. moreover, while the international labour organization estimates that 215 million children are forced to work worldwide, more than 250,000 work on cocoa plantations in west africa, and 15,000 are enslaved. the reason is simple: farmers cannot make a profit from their work, so they are forced to look for cheap labour. we must therefore demand effective legislation from the commission to combat forced child labour in the cocoa sector and put pressure on all actors in the supply chain so that farmers can be paid fairly for their work.
for the greens/efa group in its resolution of 15 september 2011, in the run-up to the un summit on non-communicable diseases, the european parliament already indicated that europe has a strong interest in a comprehensive approach and should also complement the policies of the competent authorities in the member states. we also organised a joint meeting in the european parliament in october 2010 of the diabetes working group, the heart group, the members against cancer and the meps' group on kidney health, which i chair, who also stressed the importance of coordinated action. prevention is essential and possible because the risk factors for all these diseases are known. and from that point of view, i am a little disappointed that this house has partly abandoned the common approach and has worked out a specific resolution on diabetes. the added value of last year's resolution escapes me a little, because it also ignores other non-communicable diseases such as cardiovascular disease, cancer and respiratory diseases. but i have no problem with the content of the resolution, and we will support it.
mr president, you mentioned that my country was really in shock because of this terrible bus accident and, as i speak, the parents of the children are gathered in a school and do not yet know whether their child has died or not because we do not yet have the identities. i don't think the country has ever had an accident like this. you said it yourself: 22 children were killed, the escorts, the bus driver, and there are wounded in very serious condition. i would like to thank - because i shall not have the opportunity to do so this afternoon - president martin schulz, who telephoned me at 7.15 this morning to say that he shared the sadness of the belgians and that he would observe a minute's silence at noon, when there will be more people in this chamber. thank you again for your compassion.

in writing. in 1988, europe banned imports of beef from animals treated with growth hormones. in 1999 the united states and canada, with the backing of the wto, imposed customs sanctions on many european products (such as chocolate) in retaliation. the ep has today approved a regulation increasing by 20,000 tonnes (currently set at 48,200 tonnes) the annual community tariff quota for imports into the eu of high-quality us and canadian beef from cattle not treated with growth hormones. the agreement allows the eu to maintain the ban on imports of hormone-treated cattle in exchange for increased import quotas for high-quality beef from the us and canada and the removal of sanctions on certain european products. this is therefore a crucial step towards ending a dispute that has lasted for more than a decade. i welcome this because it is a victory for european diplomacy, but above all it is a victory for respect for european consumers! the united states and canada have failed to impose hormones on us, the europeans did not want to!
in writing. the report on the autonomous tariff quota for imports of high-quality beef was approved by the house at noon. the term is not very evocative, but it is essential in that it puts an end to the war between the "hormone ox" and the "hormone cow". a relief for european consumers, farmers and distributors, bringing to an end 20 years of trade disputes between the old and the new continent! condemned by the world trade organisation for refusing to import hormone-treated beef from the united states and canada and subjected to trade retaliation measures worth millions of euros each year, the european union has always refused to compromise on its principles, considering that european consumers cannot be deceived about the quality of imported meat. the agreement allows for the permanent lifting of sanctions in exchange for import quotas of high-quality, hormone-free beef from the us and canada. a win-win solution for farmers and consumers, in europe and america, and one that takes the hormone-laden beef off our plates for good!
the european parliament has voted in favour of the commission's proposal to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'commercial vehicle' to cover vehicles with a maximum mass exceeding 10 tonnes. the trade dispute arose from the total ban on the use of hormones in livestock farming in the european union. the us and canada are now allowed to import 48,200 tonnes of high-quality non-hormone treated beef into the eu free of duty every year. in return, the almost 100 million euro of trade sanctions imposed on agricultural products annually will be removed forever. the agreement is of great political significance. the eu maintained the hormone ban and its own food standards. despite a wto condemnation, the eu market is not supplied with hormone-treated meat from the usa and canada. all imported meat is fattened under the supervision of eu inspectors on farms, which ensure that no hormones are applied. the agreement proves that you can consistently apply your own standards to imported agricultural products, even if they are formally contrary to wto rulings. moreover, the increase in imports of beef from the usa and canada from 21 500 tonnes per year to 48 200 tonnes will not have a negative impact on the european beef market, as the import quota represents only 0,36% of the total eu beef and veal market.
in writing. by deciding to set up a special committee on organised crime, corruption and money laundering, meps have become aware that the problem of the mafia, crime and money laundering must be dealt with not only at national level but also at european and international level. it's a strong message that's being sent to criminal organizations. in a europe which is fighting for respect for human rights, we had to tackle this kind of intolerable medieval practice which is rampant on european soil. it is time we took effective measures to prevent mafia organizations from entering the legal economy and/or infiltrating political circles. the new commission, which will assess the scale of organised crime, corruption and money laundering and its impact on the european union and its member states, will take measures to ensure that the european union does not leave these crimes unpunished or beyond its control.
in writing. while it is important to encourage international cooperation within the global cocoa economy, to strengthen the economies of member countries, to ensure fair prices for both producers and consumers, we cannot ignore european values, including human rights and therefore children's rights. children may not be used as forced labour on cocoa plantations under any circumstances. the european union must promote responsible development and a smart economy by promoting children's education.
in writing. the main commodity agreement between cocoa exporters and importers, the international cocoa agreement aims to make global cocoa trade more equitable and sustainable. the new agreement, however, did not address the problem of child labour. this is why parliament adopted a resolution calling for action to be taken against the use of child labour in this sector. cocoa farming is a labour-intensive activity: 90% of the world's cocoa is grown by 5.5 million smallholders, and 14 million rural workers depend directly on its production, including children. the resolution calls on all parties in the cocoa value chain to assume their responsibilities in the fight against forced labour and child trafficking in the sector. parliament also called on the commission to consider draft legislation to combat cocoa products produced by child labour effectively and to encourage the signatory countries to the unctad agreement to take action to ensure traceability throughout the supply chain.
in writing. diabetes is one of the most widespread non-communicable diseases, affecting more than 32 million eu citizens. diabetes is a major issue in our society. in a decade, the number of people with diabetes has exploded. unfortunately, there is currently no cure for diabetes. in addition, many diabetics are discriminated against and prejudice remains widespread in schools, employment agencies, the workplace and insurance policies, which sometimes charge exorbitant premiums for proper coverage. it is urgent that the commission develops and implements a targeted union strategy on diabetes and supports the action of the member states. member states should develop national programmes and give priority to the prevention of type 2 diabetes and obesity through strategies to be implemented from an early age through education on healthy eating habits and physical exercise in schools. the patient must have access to health care and diabetes-related treatments.
in writing. on 29 february, the committee on the environment, public health and food safety unanimously adopted my report on the new regulation on specific foods. it was an opportunity to recall the obvious: the 32 million european citizens with diabetes must be the object of our full attention, but this does not necessarily require specific dietary standards. on the other hand, an eu-wide strategy to combat diabetes is essential, which is expected to increase by 16% by 2030 as obesity becomes endemic and the european population ages. the resolution adopted this afternoon on this non-communicable disease, which is responsible for 325 000 deaths in europe each year, is welcome. or would it be more accurate to talk about two diseases? while the risk factors for type 2 diabetes, such as poor or unbalanced diet, obesity, lack of physical activity, and alcohol consumption, have been clearly identified, we are a long way from the risk factors for type 1 diabetes. although some characteristics seem to be established: genetic cause, early onset and emergence of the disease mainly between 5 and 15 years. this is why we need more and more research funding and results on diabetes.
in writing. since 2010, the european council has decided to open the negotiating cycle for iceland's accession to the eu. the european parliament adopted a resolution on the 2011 report of these negotiations. iceland already cooperates extensively with the eu, as the country is already part of the european economic area and the schengen area, among other things. from an economic point of view, the country's situation is slowly but surely recovering. politically, the country has a long tradition of democracy and cooperation with northern europe and is very active in transatlantic cooperation. the majority support of the icelandic population for the continuation of the accession negotiations is a clear signal of the will of the icelandic people to move closer to european standards in all matters. it was also important to recall that the settlement of the icesave issue must be settled outside the accession negotiations and cannot constitute an obstacle to iceland's accession process.
in writing. iceland fulfils the copenhagen criteria and accession negotiations have been open since july 2010. a country with a democratic tradition, it already cooperates closely with the european union: it is a member of the european economic area, it has acceded to the schengen agreements and the dublin ii regulation. it has also already incorporated whole sections of the community acquis. while the accession of iceland still raises political divisions within the government, parliament and all the main political forces in the country, a significant proportion of icelanders remain in favour of continuing the negotiations. i welcome iceland's willingness to open all the negotiating chapters during the danish presidency and hope that the negotiations will continue successfully. however, divergences remain between the union and iceland as regards whaling and fishing. i believe that iceland's accession to the union would greatly broaden the union's prospects of playing a more active and constructive role in northern europe and the arctic region.
in writing. i welcome the resolution adopted by the european parliament on the 2011 report on bosnia and herzegovina. i welcome the political stabilisation of the country through the establishment of a national government capable of implementing the many reforms needed to continue on the path to accession. the challenges to be met are many, notably in relation to the country's economic situation with a high rate of youth unemployment, the reduction of bureaucratic burdens and the fight against corruption. in this context, parliament urged the new government to give priority to the reforms needed for bosnia and herzegovina's accession to the world trade organisation in order to encourage an even more positive business climate and attract more foreign investment. however, it should be noted that the country has cooperated very well with the international criminal tribunal for the former yugoslavia, whose requests are answered promptly and appropriately. the efforts to resolve the outstanding issues between bosnia-herzegovina, serbia and croatia, which have intensified in recent months, are proof of the willingness of bosnia. volunteering is something to be encouraged.

on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, it is true, mr minister, that it is difficult for nigeria to imagine such a contrast, a very explosive cocktail of tensions. on the one hand, there is the extreme poverty of the people, especially in the north, and on the other hand, the immense oil wealth, which is exploited by companies that do not hesitate to pollute the waters of niger. we also have sectarian conflicts between the islamic north and the christian south, the presence of boko haram and therefore of hardline terrorism, which has caused two tragic deaths of hostages. there is still political corruption. there is social unrest against the backdrop of poverty, but you spoke - that's right - of the beginning of the emergence of a democracy, of nigerians taking charge of their own destiny. yet, violence is still their daily lot. the paradox of all this is that you actually see the role -- and you've described it well -- that nigeria plays at the international level and at the african level, in west africa in particular. it helps - you said it - to promote ecowas, which is based in abuja. it has increased its efforts to resolve conflicts. he has conducted mediation meetings in recent years in sudan, the democratic republic of congo and zimbabwe. he is involved in many un peacekeeping and un operations in africa. he even recently deployed an all-female police force to liberia. the nigerian people are not the only ones who are suffering from the effects of the war in the region. we ask that oil companies be required to be transparent, respect the environment and clean up the damage they have caused to the environment. there are whole amnesty international reports on the issue. we have called for the repeal of the law criminalising homosexuality, and then - i say this emphatically - it is a priority for my group to release the imprisoned trade unionists and, finally, to ensure a more equitable distribution of wealth and religious tolerance, intolerance being the breeding ground for terrorism.
the european parliament has called for the commission to take action to combat piracy. last year there were as many as 300 attacks and attempted attacks on ships in an area which a few years ago was confined to the horn of africa, the gulf of aden, but which now covers almost the entire western indian ocean. in the process, 25 ships were effectively hijacked and over 400 crew members were held captive. we also count 11 fatalities. in the first two months of this year there have been 35 attempted hijackings and three ships have been effectively hijacked. this situation leads to crew members risking their lives to carry out their work. but also that there is considerable economic damage, directly and indirectly, in the form of ransom payments, higher insurance premiums, detours to avoid dangerous areas, the operation atalanta itself, of course, higher food prices, etc., costs which all add up to many billions. consequently, additional and sustained action is required. although operation atalanta was extended until the end of 2014, in a few years the number of vessels actively contributing to the operation has been reduced from 35 to 10 and even to three vessels at the beginning of this year. the fight against piracy, of course, requires a global approach - you yourself have referred to this - in somalia itself, in the region. we also see clearly that the pressure on shipping companies is increasing day by day to ensure the safety of ships and their crews themselves through private security companies. so i think we should look at that. some eu flag states allow this, others do not. it is desirable that the european commission should come up with guidelines to regulate this clearly, for example by agreeing on training criteria for security agencies, procedures for being able to act armed, recording incidents, the type of weapons that can be kept on board. i would ask you to say something about this and to clarify your view. i would also ask you not to wait for the imo but to take action on this point and, of course, on other points which i am sure will be raised by other members.
i have to say, madam president, we are not new to piracy in this house. we had a resolution in 2009. we in the committee on transport do not want to make this a procedural issue either. we want to help people and we are quite willing to come together with the other committees who are concerned and can help to draw up a resolution. so, this is our approach, which we have also decided. the first thing that piracy on the high seas is a threat to people. as the honourable member has said before, it is also a threat to foreign trade, but also to local fisheries. that's another one to remember. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it must also be noted that in recent years, precisely because of this threat, this profession - which is a very interesting profession, even internationally recognized - has suffered greatly because of the threat to the local people. that is why we first of all want, and this was already included in the 2009 resolution, that the eu should take the initiative. the imo - with all due respect - is too lazy and too slow for us. we must take the initiative. in the legal framework, in certification, so that people who guarantee safety tomorrow do not end up in situations like the two colleagues from italy today, and tomorrow you will also have an uncertain framework there. so we have to tackle this problem, show more initiative at eu level, and then bring this to the international stage. that's what we expect. that is also something we want to discuss with the other committees without any problems.
we have a very important report before us and it seems to me very important that we send a clear signal, but that is still a little difficult to do here, if i hear the right side of the house. the council is not taking clear decisions today, and that has everything to do with the arguments i hear there. let me repeat - and perhaps the commissioner should bring this back to the council's discussions - that this is an important issue not only from the point of view of climate and global warming, but also from the economic point of view. by 2050, that economy has to be low carbon, there's no alternative. the question is whether we can make the decisions today, provide the legal framework, establish the right regulation, so that our industry has the certainty to invest, our governments have the means to do what needs to be done. it just takes courage! it is not worth arguing today that the time is not right and that the economy must be saved. the long-term economy needs to be saved, and that has everything to do with that low-carbon economy. i therefore hope, and i look to the people of good sense on the right, that we will soon decide unanimously and give the council and the rest of the world a clear signal, because that is what we will need to give our children a future too.
in writing. i am convinced that we as the eu are on the right track by making a strong commitment to a low-carbon and carbon-neutral society. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we are 500 million in the eu, but unfortunately that is not enough to stop global warming on our own. we need to get china, india, the united states and other emerging countries on this path. that is why it is so important that five american nobel prize winners yesterday issued a very clear call to president obama to support the eu's measures to combat co2 emissions from aircraft. the cost of this measure is around 2 euros per ticket. we must not abandon this endeavour, because it obliges the other great powers to take a stand for their own public opinion and to make clear where they stand in the fight against global warming. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of the environment.

in writing. - i am in favour of the ambitious targets set by europe in the field of environment and energy. however, europe's pioneering role must not lead to its external isolation. the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions is essential and the regulations must have a pan-european impact, because air does not stop at the borders of europe. however, the specifications should be negotiated in consultation and agreement with third countries, in particular where those specifications have a direct impact on them. the european emissions trading system is a good example of how international opposition to a regulation agreed at european level can be met just before it enters into force. europe should stick to its policy but seek international dialogue. the ehs should be a compromise, above all at icao level, which would also allow third countries to accept the ehs.

in writing. the european parliament adopted a much-needed resolution on kazakhstan following the violent crackdown on protesters last december which resulted in many deaths. following these events, the kazakh authorities declared a state of emergency and the independent press was subjected to attacks and intimidation, which still prevents the clarification of events. in general, there has been a deterioration in respect for human rights in the country for some time. it is important that the european parliament should make its voice heard and firmly condemn the current deviations. we therefore call on the kazakh authorities to spare no effort to improve the human rights situation on their soil and stress that progress in the negotiations on the new enhanced partnership and cooperation agreement between the european union and kazakhstan must depend on the implementation of political reforms and a significant improvement in the human rights situation in the country.
in writing. the declaration made today by the european parliament on the occasion of the 6th world water forum in marseille is symbolically important: water is a common good of humanity and should not be a source of illegitimate profit. among the reasons for satisfaction: the achievement of one of the 8 millennium development goals (ensuring a sustainable environment): 89% of the world's population now has access to water, more than 6 billion people connected to a drinking water network or who can access uncontaminated wells. however, there is cause for concern: nearly 800 million people still lack access to clean drinking water. what's europe got to do with it? in view of the scarcity of water resources and recurrent droughts, it is obvious that it must secure its water supply through rational use in agriculture and in the urban environment. other ways to do this include increasing the area of woodland to protect soil and water, and using good agricultural practices to combat the excessive use of water contaminants (nitrogen, phosphorus and pesticides) which have harmful effects on human health and the environment.
